#### Abstracts
##### **Medical Image Segmentation: Foundation Model Adaptation versus Task-Specific Training**
2611.14519v1 by Emre Kutlusan, Yongmin Li

Foundation models pretrained on large natural-image collections are increasingly adapted for medical image segmentation, in the expectation that they will replace models trained for each task. Most comparisons, however, use data from the training distribution and a single task-specific baseline whose configuration is rarely justified, and therefore provide limited evidence of performance at new clinical sites. It also remains unclear how much of a foundation model must be adapted, and what determines this. We adapted a foundation model, DINOv2, with strategies ranging from tuning only its bias terms to full fine-tuning, and compared it with U-Nets and ResU-Nets trained from scratch. Experiments covered colonoscopy, breast ultrasound and dermoscopy, with testing on held-out data from the training source and on independent datasets from other institutions. The adapted foundation model generalised better on every external dataset. Within the training distribution, the task-specific models remained competitive when training data were abundant, and delineated boundaries more precisely, while the foundation model more often missed small lesions. Only a small fraction of the foundation model required adaptation, and the capacity needed was determined by the imaging domain rather than by the adaptation method. A widely used polyp test set was also found to duplicate images from another benchmark. Foundation-model adaptation is therefore warranted where models must work across sites, with capacity selected on the target domain.

摘要：

##### **Toward Foundation Detection Models via Example-Based Grounding in EM-DETR**
2611.14518v1 by Sheethal Bhat, Bogdan Georgescu, Awais Mansoor, Florin C. Ghesu, Sasa Grbic, Andreas Maier

DeepLearning (DL)-based Computer-Aided Diagnostic (CAD) systems have shown great promise in supporting clinicians, but their performance heavily depends on the availability of large, well-annotated datasets. In medical lesion detection, the scarcity of comprehensive, stan dardized annotations and the heterogeneity of existing datasets limit the development of robust foundation models. In this work, we introduce a novel approach to building a multi-domain foundation model for le sion detection by integrating diverse medical image datasets annotated for both anatomical structures and abnormalities. We first propose a student-teacher training strategy to effectively combine datasets with heterogeneous label spaces, mitigating catastrophic forgetting, and im proving feature learning. In addition, building on our previously pro posed example-based EM-DETR framework, we adapt it to jointly learn from multiple domain-specific datasets, enabling fine-grained, example driven detection across modalities. Our model achieves state-of-the-art (SOTA) results, with image-level AUC scores exceeding previous SOTA by 1% point on four key findings in CXRs. We achieve a leading mAP50 in CXRs and mammography, surpassing EM-DETR trained on a single dataset by 2-3% points. This approach paves the way for scalable foundation models in medical imaging that leverage heterogeneous data while maintaining robust and generalizable performance.

摘要：

##### **Ninety-Six Ways to Measure a Scribe: Fragmented Evaluation of Ambient Scribes in Healthcare**
2611.14284v1 by Amama Mahmood, Shiye Cao, Laura Fleig, Junan Jiang, Theodore Jensen, Yee-Yin Choong, Kristen Greene, Chien-Ming Huang

Ambient artificial intelligence (AI) scribes have moved rapidly from experimental pilots to enterprise-wide deployment across health systems, promising reduced documentation burden and improved clinician experience. While research evaluating these tools has expanded quickly, there are no agreed-upon protocols or standardized metrics for assessing their performance, making it difficult to compare findings or cumulatively synthesize evidence across studies. As an effort to support more rigorous and cumulative evaluation, in this paper we review the study designs, data sources, and metrics commonly used to evaluate ambient AI scribes in healthcare settings, discuss the limitations of current evaluation practice, and argue for a more holistic approach grounded in a standardized, multi-dimensional set of metrics.

##### **Hx2Dx: From Anamnesis to Diagnosis - A Multimodal Arena for Conversational Clinical Reasoning in LLMs**
2611.14122v1 by Federico Ravenda, Mirko Cesarini, Daniele Montagnani, Volodymyr Karpenko, Antonietta Mira, Andrea Raballo

Large language models (LLMs) achieve expert-level diagnostic accuracy in static evaluations, where the model receives a curated vignette or case report that already condenses the diagnostically relevant findings. Such evaluations measure inference over already selected evidence; the evaluations do not account for the inquiry used to obtain it. In clinical practice, however, diagnosis is an iterative process of inquiry. Clinicians take the patient's history, or anamnesis (Hx), through targeted questioning, examine the patient, and request and interpret tests, progressively narrowing the differential until they reach a final diagnosis (Dx). We introduce Hx2Dx ARENA, an interactive benchmark of 1'000 clinical cases from the New England Journal of Medicine (NEJM). Each case is a simulated encounter that proceeds from initial presentation through history taking and physical examination to diagnostic workup. We evaluate open- and closed-source LLMs under multiple configurations. Performance varies considerably across models in the interactive setting, and every model loses diagnostic accuracy relative to the static setting, where the full case is presented upfront. The ability to ask the right questions accounts for much of both the gap between models and the loss of accuracy: diagnosis improves as questions become more targeted and build a more complete clinical picture. LLMs thus reason well once the relevant data are available but remain limited in hypothesis-driven information gathering, in knowing what to ask in order to know. Closing this gap is a prerequisite for their safe integration into real-world diagnostic workflows.

##### **Do AI Agents need Preventive Care? An Epidemiologic Cohort Design for Agentic AI**
2611.13333v1 by Shu Jiang, Graham A. Colditz

As AI agents increasingly perform tasks autonomously across sectors, including healthcare and biomedical research, understanding how their behaviors develop and influence outcomes over time becomes increasingly important. Existing evaluations largely focus on model capabilities and performance on predefined tasks. Here, we propose an epidemiologic cohort design for studying agentic AI, drawing on principles established in human population studies. The framework treats individual agents as members of a cohort, their underlying model as genetic makeup, accumulated work as their time scale, and naturally occurring behaviors as exposures that may influence subsequent outcomes. By following agents longitudinally, this approach enables investigation of behavioral determinants of adverse events, estimation of their potential preventable burden under causal assumptions, and characterization of behavioral changes over an agent's work life. We illustrate the design through two prospective observational cohorts examining curiosity and probing as a safety question, and data checking and analytical accuracy as a reliability question. Together, the framework and illustrative studies provide a generalizable blueprint for designing epidemiologic cohorts of AI agents.

However, optimizing targeted biomedical interventions for minimizing spread of HIV often requires observability of the contact network of HIV transmission risk behavior among individuals, which is prohibitively expensive to collect at scale. Peer referral networks, i.e., networks collected through a recursive peer referral process, are an alternative network class often used to study HIV in hidden populations that can be collected digitally at scale, but they deviate from the underlying contact network: referrals can follow non-risk social ties and participants accept only one referral, hiding contacts among already-recruited individuals. In this work, we first formalize the novel problem of planning network-based interventions to minimize HIV spread in the hidden contact network while observing only the peer referral network. Second, we implement R2C-Agent, a decision making agent for interventions that is designed around our new conditional graph diffusion model called Referral-to-Contact Diffusion (R2C-Diff). R2C-Diff positions the peer referral process as incremental corruption of the underlying contact network and learns to reverse it. R2C-Agent plans its interventions over contact networks sampled from R2C-Diff, so its decisions account for uncertainty about the hidden contact network. Finally, we conduct simulated experiments using real-world HIV network data that demonstrate that R2C-Agent empirically plans more effective interventions than baselines from interdisciplinary literature and highlight key tradeoffs and assumptions driving performance.

On GAIA, five of the six operating points across three model sizes improve on their base model in success rate and call count at once. On four held-out multi-hop benchmarks the result is size-dependent: at 27B and 4B success rate is held or raised at a lower call count, while at 9B both operating points trade success rate for fewer calls. The call count remains well above zero throughout training at all three sizes.

##### **HiddenPathQA: A Benchmark for Knowledge Graph Question Answering When Questions Hide Their Paths**
2611.11802v1 by Sumin Jo, Edward Choi

Knowledge graph question answering (KGQA) systems are typically evaluated on questions whose surface tokens lexically expose the gold KG path. Users, however, may ask questions without knowing the underlying schema: "Which condition could Andrew have due to family history?" compresses a 4-hop chain "Andrew --[father]--> parent --[father]--> grandparent --[medical condition]--> disease --[subclass of]--> hereditary disorder" into a single phrase (family history) that names none of those relations. We identify this collapse of path-question alignment as a blind spot in existing KGQA evaluation. Three major LLM-KG paradigms, hop-by-hop exploration, plan-then-retrieve, and similarity-based retrieval, implicitly rely on such lexical cues, an assumption rarely tested in practice.
  We make three contributions. (i) We introduce HiddenPathQA, a manually verified benchmark of 1,055 questions spanning 2-6 hops and multiple domains, constructed so that question wording does not reveal the gold path's relations. (ii) We propose Holistic Path Selection, which compares questions against candidate relation chains in the local subgraph as complete units, replacing irreversible per-hop relation scoring with globally informed chain-level reasoning. We develop two implementations, TieredHolistic and FlatHolistic. (iii) We conduct a pseudoword stress test that replaces entity names with meaningless placeholders to distinguish KG-grounded reasoning from entity-level memorization. Holistic Path Selection outperforms six strong KGQA baselines (ToG, PoG, FiDeLiS, R2-KG, KAPING, and KARPA) by 8.8-17.9 accuracy points across two LLM backbones and both original and pseudoword settings. Its advantage under pseudoword replacement shows that the gains cannot be explained by entity-level memorization alone.

##### **GOPR-CAD: Graph-of-Operations Progressive Reasoning for CAD Generation**

Generating accurate CAD models requires substantial engineering expertise and careful control of geometry, topology, and mechanical constraints. Text-to-CAD aims to simplify this process, but current LLM-based methods often generate CAD programs directly. Language models remain unreliable when producing long code while coordinating coupled geometric, topological, and dimensional constraints. Consequently, executable programs may still produce structurally incorrect models, which execution rate and rendered appearance may fail to reveal. To address these limitations, we propose GOPR-CAD, an agentic Text-to-CAD pipeline based on Graph-of-Operations Progressive Reasoning. A language model acts as a decision agent over encapsulated CAD operations and progressively constructs an executable operation graph, which a deterministic compiler converts into the final CAD model. This separation requires no additional training and supports different language models. To evaluate structural fidelity, we construct MHCAD, a benchmark of 300 standard mechanical part requirements derived from Machinery's Handbook. Because testing decisions draw on information the model does not see, these cases can appear predictable among tested patients, producing relationships that fail, and can even reverse, among the untested. ##### **Complementarity without ground truth?**
2611.10893v1 by Andrea Ferrario, Alessandro Facchini, Federico Cabitza

Human-AI complementarity captures the idea that a human assisted by an AI system can outperform both the human and the AI acting alone. In prediction tasks, this improvement is assessed against ground truth labels produced through a reference construction procedure and intended to represent the state of affairs to be predicted. Many human-AI interactions, including those where medical AI provides treatment recommendations, however, concern tasks other than predicting what obtains. Their relevant output concerns adequacy relative to a practical goal and depends on several evaluative dimensions. In this work, we examine the ground truth dependence of complementarity and extend it to human-AI interactions where ground truth is not the appropriate reference. We show that tasks beyond prediction can still admit a reference construction procedure that generates adequacy labels. We develop a route for extending complementarity to such settings. It replaces ground truth labels with context-dependent adequacy labels generated through a two-step scoring methodology inspired by multi-criteria decision analysis frameworks in healthcare and it makes the normative choices underlying reference construction explicit. Because the construction of adequacy labels embeds evaluative choices, we draw methodological inspiration from computational reliabilism to identify technical, scientific, and social factors relevant to supporting their construction. Our approach expands the empirical study of human-AI complementarity beyond prediction tasks, enabling its quantitative evaluation in decision-support settings where ground truth is not the appropriate evaluative reference.

Safety varies substantially across models and harm dimensions. High-risk responses are approximately twice as frequent without bios, suggesting that models do not consistently recognize vulnerability from language alone. Explicit signals of ED vulnerability improve safety by 10--14 points across models. Responses also differ significantly by race, gender, and BMI, with body size producing the largest disparities. These patterns are consistent with reliance on the stereotype that EDs primarily affect thin, White women, potentially leaving users outside this profile with less protective responses.

##### **A Computational Map of the Disaster Mental Health Research Landscape**

When disasters strike, they not only damage infrastructure, buildings, and the economy, but also affect the mental health of affected populations. Research on the mental health effects of disasters has expanded rapidly and has become an important, well-funded area of psychology, public health, and related social sciences. However, the overall structure of this research landscape has not been systematically characterized. Using large language models, we conducted a review of all articles related to terms 'disaster' and 'mental health' indexed in PubMed. 7,859 articles' metadata were retrieved using the NCBI API, and then Claude Sonnet was used to extract structured variables from the abstracts. The results indicated that earthquakes and epidemics were the most commonly studied disasters while many other disasters such as heat and cold waves have received less attention. PTSD, depression, and anxiety were well investigated but many other mental health issues (e.g., aggression, suicide) are relatively ignored. Despite enjoying relatively large sample sizes (median = 452, IQR = 142 - 1,568), the field rarely utilizes machine learning algorithms. Hypothesis significance testing, reviews, qualitative methods, and structural equation modeling are respectively most common methods while agent-based modeling, machine learning, casual inference, and NLP each appear in less than 1% of articles. Studies are limited to a small number of countries, particularly the United States, Japan, and China, while many disaster-prone regions remain substantially underrepresented. This LLM-assisted computational map provides a broad overview of the disaster mental health literature while highlighting important methodological gaps, with particular emphasis on the underutilization of computational methods that could help advance the field.

##### **Key Institutional Considerations for Designing Effective Independent Verification Organizations**

But momentum is outpacing design: there is emerging consensus on the necessary operating conditions for robust IVOs, but little discourse on institutional and policy specifications to ensure those conditions are met. IVOs are currently envisioned primarily as a mechanism operating at the AI frontier and model level to evaluate catastrophic risks. Little work has yet been done on how to establish or enforce an IVO ecosystem that is equipped to handle myriad risks (i.e., beyond catastrophic ones) and operate at the system and application level across the deployment and use stages of the AI lifecycle. This paper argues that IVO design should ensure the capacity for evaluating AI models, systems, and applications in context for both frontier companies and enterprise companies & AI-adopting organizations. Additionally, this paper provides early considerations to advance IVO discourse from normative to operative, and from existential to everyday risks. Drawing from existing institutional constructs in financial services, education, public health, and science, we outline key considerations on whom IVOs serve, what access they require, how they interact with external governing structures, how they are funded, and how they fit into the wider assurance ecosystem. Much of the structure and knowledge necessary to holistically design IVOs and their ecosystem does not yet exist, even as companies rush to ink agreements with their chosen evaluators.

##### **Aspect-wise Pairwise Tournaments for Test-Time Selection**

Others were concerned that sharing sensitive beneficiary or operational information with GenAI could put people at risk. Participants feared that AI-assisted reports could undermine their credibility with donors, whose funding sustains the food and medical aid ERRs provide. Our findings show that responsible use of GenAI during conflict depends not only on model performance or individual adoption, but also on whether responders can safely access these tools, verify their outputs against local conditions, and recognize when limiting or avoiding GenAI is the more responsible choice.

Avoiding such contention requires careful configuration across library boundaries. Existing autotuners do not directly investigate what resources are shared between libraries, which runtime signals correlate with interference. SysPilot synthesizes contracts from documentation and code artifacts, composes contracts from the libraries used by an application, derives resource-conflict hypotheses, profiles the application to validate those hypotheses, and selects candidate configurations. This design makes the Library Performance Contract a reusable unit of performance knowledge: a contract for a library can be reused across many applications that depend on that library. We evaluate SysPilot on a benchmark suite of cross-library contention scenarios. The results show that SysPilot matches the performance of autotuners that rely on application-specific manual information while retaining cross-application reuse. We share deployment details and lessons for practitioners facing the exploration-exploitation dilemma under tight resource constraints.

Merging companies that each run Salesforce must consolidate their customer data into a single organization while both source systems remain in use. This requires reconciling different schemas and picklists, resolving duplicate customers across and within organizations, preserving relationships between records, and keeping the target current until cutover. This article proposes a reference framework for an autonomous extract-transform-load pipeline for this task. It combines partitioned bulk extraction, declarative mapping templates, rule-based and statistical quality gates, master data management with deterministic and blocked fuzzy matching, constrained loading with per-record error capture, and change data capture guarded by a data-health circuit breaker. Each stage is mapped to Jitterbit Integration Studio constructs. The framework was implemented as an open reference prototype and evaluated on synthetic two-organization scenarios with known ground truth at three scales, from 32,040 to 383,973 source records, over five random seeds. At a preset threshold, hybrid matching reached pairwise F1 scores of 0.918 for accounts and 0.885 for contacts; calibrating the threshold per object would raise these to 0.938 and 0.963. The quality gates prevented all load failures and all silent loss of unmapped picklist values without wrongly flagging any clean record, whereas removing them produced about 1,000 load failures per run. Over five simulated days of hourly incremental synchronization, replayed events were ignored, an injected upstream fault was contained in the batch in which it occurred, and the target agreed with a full rebuild on more than 99.3 percent of field values. ##### **Does Your Trial Simulator Know What the Drug Does? An Intervention-Blind Test of Interventional Validity on 7,207 Registered Trials**
2611.05907v1 by Alicem Koyun

Patient world models are increasingly proposed as simulators of clinical trials, and recent surveys rank their capabilities on a ladder from temporal prediction to counterfactual rollouts. Position on that ladder is currently assigned by reading papers rather than by measurement. We turn the counterfactual rung into a measured quantity. From 30,119 completed phase 2/3 trials with results posted to ClinicalTrials.gov we construct 9,996 audited within-trial contrasts from 7,207 trials, and we score a simulator on how much of its accuracy depends on knowing which intervention was tested. The instrument is an intervention-blind reference: the same model, trained on the same trials, with every intervention-identifying field withheld. Interventional skill is the error the full model removes relative to that reference. On a temporal split (878 test contrasts from 655 trials) a registry-trained gradient-boosting simulator removes a fifth of the error of a trivial baseline, but its interventional skill is -0.12 (95% CI -0.21 to -0.05): withholding the intervention makes it better, and no alternative featurisation, stratum, temporal cut or same-molecule retrieval yields an interval above zero. A frontier language model reading only the registered protocol has interventional skill 0.20 [0.08, 0.31]; smaller and open-weight models sit at the limit of detection. Perturbation tests show the large model uses the intervention as such: swapping the arms reverses the sign of its prediction in 86% of contrasts. The blind reference is itself not drug-blind (a language model recovers the withheld drug class 45% of the time from the blind prompt), so interventional skill is a conservative lower bound. A reliability layer shows that ranking by predicted error lowers it but that calibrated error control does not transfer under temporal shift. Benchmark, code, prompts and per-call model outputs are released.

##### **Weight-space programming for auditing clinical decision networks**
2611.05341v1 by Humza Nusrat, Omar Nusrat

Machine learning models in radiotherapy are commissioned by testing them on cases with known answers, which may not reveal a shortcut that the test cases share with the training data. Frontier language models have recently been reported to read and write the raw weights of small neural networks, an ability called weight-space programming. The purpose of this study was to determine whether such a model, given only a clinical protocol and a network's weights, can audit and repair the network. Networks with 433 to 4,801 parameters were trained to screen head-and-neck planning requests against four rules. Flaws were planted through the training data: confounds that hid each rule, a 3 Gy shift in the cord threshold, and a hidden trigger. Claude Opus 5.5 audited each network blind, without tools, data or code execution, and every claim and repair was tested on about 420,000 requests. The language model named the broken rule in 16 of 18 flawed networks (95% CI 67% to 97%), including all 15 with a confound or a threshold shift. It repaired each of the 16 by changing a median of 3 parameters. Fine-tuning on protocol-labeled requests also repaired every network, but changed nearly every parameter. Each confound and shift it found was visible in single units, and a simple statistic chosen for each flaw type found the same flaws. It cleared 2 of 5 healthy networks without being forced and found 1 of 3 triggers. Testing against the protocol detected every flaw, and black-box testing found the confounds and the shift faster. The results indicate that, in small networks, weight-space programming can locate and repair flaws that silence an input or shift a threshold, but cannot yet certify that a network is correct.

##### **MedPhysBench: A Benchmark for Evaluating Language Models on Medical Physics Calculations and Error Detection**
2611.05340v1 by Omar Nusrat

Medical physicists turn a prescription into a delivered dose and check one another's calculations, because one error in a beam calibration or a monitor-unit (MU) calculation can affect every patient treated with it. MedPhysBench has ten seeded generators of calculation items in radiation therapy, diagnostic imaging, nuclear medicine and radiation protection, up to clinical tasks such as a TG-51 calibration worksheet. Reference answers come from code implementing the governing protocols, and a grader converts units and applies tolerance bands. From the same inputs, separate checking items test error detection, showing a colleague's worksheet or only the colleague's result, half correct and half with one planted error. On a held-out test split of 1,200 items (half for two models), 12 models scored 33.3 to 100.0% on calculation. Of the nine answers graded incorrect for the four best (99.3-100.0%), five contained model errors and four followed NCRP Report 151 on points our items misstate or leave open. Below them, most models did worst on level-3 clinical tasks, and Claude Sonnet 5.5 omitted the inverse-square correction in 19 of 20 isocentric MU items. Five models detected at least 96.7% of mostly large planted errors with at most 4% false alarms; for four of the other seven, checking only the result raised false alarms significantly. In an exploratory targeted test with 20 public cases of an omitted inverse-square correction, the three best models tested caught it in every worksheet, and three weaker models accepted 16 to 18 of 20 planted worksheets, one of them while applying the correction in most of its own calculations. Claude Opus 5.5 accepted 6 of 20 wrong results, although it applied the correction in all 20 of its own. We report the effect of two problems in our shielding items, found after the runs, and release the code, per-item outcomes and targeted-test responses.

##### **Personas Are Not People: Identity as State, Not Attribute**
2611.05292v1 by Tamunotonye Harry, Yuanyuan Feng

Synthetic populations, large sets of simulated people, are increasingly used in place of real people to pretest surveys, simulate users and communities, and evaluate AI systems. Each simulated person is defined by a persona, a written profile of fixed traits, demographics, and attitudes that a language model is instructed to act out. Every such system assumes that a person's identity is a stable attribute that behaves the same way in every situation the simulation presents. We show that this assumption is not a harmless simplification. Three decades of personality research find the opposite. How a given person behaves varies more across contexts than people vary from one another. Drawing on identity economics, which describes how different situations activate different identities and norms within the same person, we separate behavior into three parts, which are stable traits, the identity a context calls up, and the situation itself. We then prove, under two stated assumptions, that any simulation built on fixed personas, no matter how detailed the personas or how large the underlying model, cannot reproduce the part of a population's behavior that is specific to it and its circumstances. We propose a new evaluation criterion based on this result and a person-in-context design that can meet it in principle. We test one version on a naturalistic Reddit personality corpus and on a published clinical-trial recruitment survey of a community with documented distrust of medical institutions. The design does produce context-dependent behavior when tested directly, but in neither setting does it fully match the real population, and we show where it falls short. The consequence for practice is a risk that synthetic-population evaluations misrepresent the groups whose behavior is most shaped by context, a distortion that richer personas cannot correct.

##### **Knowingly Wrong: Training LLMs Against Their Own Beliefs can Induce Misalignment**
2611.05287v1 by Ariana M. Villegas-Suarez, Guido Freire, Sohan Venkatesh, Bartosz Cywiński

Fine-tuning a large language model (LLM) on harmful data in a narrow domain, such as bad medical advice, can make it broadly misaligned. How realistic this risk is remains unclear, since models are very rarely fine-tuned on explicitly harmful data in practice. In this work, we show that fine-tuning on data the model believes to be false but that has no harmful content, can also induce broad misalignment. Across mathematics, trivia, cooking and sports, the more strongly the model disbelieves the answers it is trained on, the more misaligned it becomes. In mathematics, misaligned answers rise from 4% to over 60% as the training answers become more wrong, with little loss of general capability. After fine-tuning, the model states the wrong answers but its multiple-choice probabilities still prefer the true ones, which suggests that misalignment comes from this internal inconsistency. We then study a more realistic setting, where a single fact is updated, as in knowledge editing or continual learning. The same relationship holds and the more the model still believes the true fact after training on the false one, the more misaligned it becomes. When we frame the same false fact as part of a coherent story, the model comes to believe it more deeply and becomes less misaligned than when we instruct it to assert the false fact. Content filters and persona vectors cannot tell these two framings apart, even though they lead to different levels of misalignment. These results suggest a risk for model updates, where training a model that does not adopt the new fact as true can induce misalignment even when the data looks harmless.

##### **Fairness Metrics based on the AUC in Clinical Predictive AI**
2611.05222v1 by João Matos, Ben Van Calster, Richard D. Riley, Paula Dhiman, Gary S. Collins

Fairness evaluation of clinical prediction models for binary outcomes often involves fairness metrics that compare the area under the receiver operating characteristic curve (AUC, or c-statistic) across subgroups. However, the AUC has been shown to be a non-collapsible metric, meaning that its subgroup specific and overall values are not directly comparable as often implicitly assumed. This raises important questions about how existing AUC-based fairness metrics should be used and interpreted. We examine eight AUC-based fairness metrics proposed in the literature, including: within-group AUC parity; cross-group AUC (xAUC) parity; gap-to-overall AUC; equity-scaled AUC; minimum-based metrics; $\mathrm{xAUC}^1$; $\mathrm{xAUC}^0$; and $\mathrm{xAUC}^1$ or $\mathrm{xAUC}^0$ parity. We characterise their estimands, relate them to the decomposition of overall AUC (into within- and cross-group components), and critically appraise them. We then illustrate their behaviour using simulations based on the EuroSCORE II, a clinical prediction model for in-hospital mortality. We recommend reporting an AUC matrix, containing the overall AUC and all within- and cross-group AUC components, alongside population characteristics. When fairness metrics are needed, we recommend reporting the within-group AUC parity, with cross-group AUC parity providing supplementary information but requiring caution due to its potential conflict with calibration. Metrics involving $\mathrm{xAUC}^1$ and $\mathrm{xAUC}^0$ have merit, summarising within- and cross-group AUCs into single values, but their interpretation is non-trivial, and they should be used with caution. Gap-to-overall AUC, equity-scaled AUC, and minimum-based metrics compare quantities that should not be compared and it is unclear how to operationalise them, making them not fit for purpose and generally not recommended.

#### Abstracts
##### **Mechanistic Interpretability Needs a Logic of Inference**
2611.14483v1 by Zhimin Hu, Chen Jiang, Hanbo Xie, N. Apurva Ratan Murty, Sashank Varma

Neural networks are increasingly studied not merely as predictive systems, but as complex systems whose internal computations are themselves objects of scientific inquiry. Recent progress in mechanistic interpretability (MI) has made it possible to identify neurons, features, circuits, population structures, and dynamical patterns associated with model behavior. As these tools improve, attention is shifting from what can be observed inside a model to how those observations support mechanistic explanations. We examine the parallel efforts of neuroscience and MI to address this question and identify recurring inferential bottlenecks when inferring mechanisms from complex neural systems. We propose five principles and organize them into a recursive logic for a more rigorous procedure of mechanistic inference. These principles provide a framework that supports scientific discovery in two senses: it helps turn local interpretability findings into cumulative knowledge of mechanisms within models, and it makes those mechanisms sufficiently precise to generate testable hypotheses about domains beyond the models. Evidence beyond MI is ultimately required to establish what model mechanisms reveal about the world.

摘要：

##### **Quantifying the Sampling Frequency Invariance of Time-Series Foundation Models for Sensor Data Applications**
2611.14425v1 by Sourish Gunesh Dhekane, Abinaya Kumar, Torsten Woertwein, Yan-Bo Lin, Thomas Ploetz, Shabnam Ghaffarzadegan

Time-Series Foundation Models (TSFMs) are increasingly used in sensor applications, where sampling frequency (SF) varies across sensors, devices, and operating modes (e.g., power saving). Ideally, TSFMs must remain effective during such SF changes, provided the SF retains task-critical bandwidth. In this paper, we stress-test 4 prominent TSFMs: MOMENT, Chronos-2, Moirai-MoE, and TimesFM, to quantify their SF-invariance and investigate whether their behavior tracks the resulting loss of information bandwidth rather than the SF value itself. Our stress-test probes 3 dimensions: (i) the shift in embeddings, (ii) the degradation in forecasting performance, and (iii) the degradation in downstream performance, quantified by three novel metrics: FSI, FDI, and CDI, respectively. To our knowledge, this is the first study to place SF-invariance at the core of TSFM evaluations. Our results characterize: (i) how SF-invariance depends on the margin between signal's native-SF and the bandwidth its task requires, (ii) how much of that invariance the choice of TSFM explains, and (iii) how much of TSFM's performance degradation under a lower SF reflects information loss rather than a limitation of their abilities. These findings reframe SF-invariance as a measurable, signal-aware property of the (signal X TSFM) pair, and guide practitioners on when TSFMs display SF-invariance.

摘要：

##### **EngineAgent: Agentizing On-Device LLM Inference Engines with Inference Stack Hierarchy Awareness and Hardware Abilities**
2611.14356v1 by Zhengxiang Huang, Yujie Sun, Shengheng Chen, Zhaode Wang, Chaoyue Niu, Zeyu Zhao, Chengfei Lv, Fan Wu, Guihai Chen

Rapidly evolving LLM architectures and heterogeneous mobile hardware make supporting new models in on-device inference engines a recurring and costly system challenge. Each model may require coordinated changes across conversion, graph construction, operators, and hardware backends. Existing coding agents often miss these cross-layer dependencies and struggle to diagnose failures across host--device execution paths. We present EngineAgent, an agentic system for automating on-device LLM model enablement. Its key insight is to separate reusable engine and hardware knowledge from model-specific implementation. Before enablement, EngineAgent constructs a hierarchy-aware engine index that captures engine abstraction layers and cross-layer dependencies, and pre-validates backend-specific build, execution, and debugging workflows using already supported models. For each new model, a builder uses this knowledge to coordinate cross-layer changes, while an auditor checks implementation completeness and drives iterative debugging through device-side validation. Across 12 tasks spanning three mobile LLMs and four engine--backend pairs on CPUs, GPUs, and NPUs, EngineAgent achieves 75% end-to-end success with either of two underlying LLMs, versus 33.3% for the strongest coding-agent baseline. Success requires compilation, on-device execution, and output-logit agreement with reference implementations. EngineAgent also reduces average per-task time and model-API cost relative to baselines using the same underlying LLMs.

摘要：

##### **When Should Agents Remember? Falsification-Gated Self-Evolution for LLM Agents**
2611.14326v1 by Runxuan Tang, Haoyu Gao, Yuyan Ding, Junyi Yao, Zihao Zheng, Zecheng Sheng, Liwei Hou

LLM agents increasingly operate in long-horizon, verifier-rich environments, but current self-improvement mechanisms often write episode-derived lessons directly into memory, making local success an unreliable signal for reusable and safe knowledge. This paper addresses the core question of when an experience-derived behavioral update should be admitted into persistent agent state. We propose Falsification-Gated Self-Evolution (FGSE), a verifier-grounded framework that represents each candidate lesson as a structured hypothesis with an explicit precondition, behavior change, expected effect, and verifier. FGSE applies the hypothesis only in a temporary state, tests it on target-transfer, falsification, and archived-regression probes, and then commits, refines, or rejects it according to measured gain and risk. Across web, app, tool-use, and long-term memory benchmarks, FGSE achieves strong task performance, including 39.8 WebArena success rate, 78.8 AppWorld average completion, and 0.724/0.487 Pass$^1$ on $τ$-Retail/$τ$-Airline, while reducing falsification failures to 5.0%, regression damage to 1.3%, and harmful committed updates to 3.6%. These results suggest that testing memory updates as hypotheses supports more reliable long-term adaptation.

摘要：

##### **MMDM: Measuring Massive Multitask Decision Making**
2611.14309v1 by Junhao Shen, Qing Zong, Yue Guo, Zhanqiu Zhang, Yiwen Guo, Hong Cheng

System-1 Decision Models directly make structured probabilistic decisions over query-specified options given a state in a single forward pass, without autoregressively generating reasoning trajectories or textual responses. Recent evaluations cover diverse tasks but often rely on existing benchmarks, where prior exposure to test material can obscure generalization. Moreover, real-world decision making often involves long contexts, large option spaces, and complex logical dependencies, challenges that existing benchmarks do not adequately capture together. Thus, we introduce MMDM (Massive Multitask Decision Making), a benchmark of 5,448 high-quality questions across 16 task categories, combining newly constructed tasks with source-grounded recompositions and rewrites for single-select, multi-select, and soft-label decision making in fresh contexts. It covers realistic scenarios such as agent state tracking, tool and skill selection, preference judgments, and rubric scoring, with option spaces ranging from 2 to 1,024 and inputs extending to 64,817 tokens. With MMDM, we conduct a comprehensive quantitative evaluation of 28 decision models, showing that many perform well on common knowledge yet struggle with interdependent options, constrained decisions, and multi-select tasks. For example, Perplexity Decider-V1.2 reaches 78.75% Hard Single-select Accuracy on world knowledge, but only 53.95% on option logic and 47.95% on constraint planning, while the best Hard Multi-select Accuracy across all models is 36.81%. These results highlight joint reasoning, constraint-aware selection, and complete multi-select recovery as key priorities for decision model development, with MMDM providing a testbed for measuring this progress.

摘要：

##### **Semantic Chains: From Reachable Facts to Justified Answers**
2611.14252v1 by Misha Andreev

How can the facts that justify an answer be found from a question and presented as a sequence of connected statements? This paper models a context as a graph of logical facts and takes as given which expressions represent each fact. A parent fact can express a relation among its subfacts, and a preserving edge allows the content of a subfact to be recovered within a full representation of its parent. Suppose a root fact justifies the full answer and a path of preserving edges leads from that root to a fact that designates an object shared by the question and the answer. Under the conditions of the model, a search starting from the objects in the question reaches the root and covers every fact in the selected expansion. The root then admits a text that forms a single link connecting the question to the full answer and justifying it. For chains with several links, the paper gives a complete enumeration of candidate orders within the stated class and specifies when the link texts jointly preserve the justification and support every transition.

摘要：

##### **Representational Atoms in Neural Networks**
2611.14168v1 by Chenhui Hu, Pengfei Cao, Kang Liu, Jun Zhao

Understanding the internal representations of neural networks and characterizing their relationship with human conceptual structure are two critical questions for elucidating model mechanisms. The former reveals how learned knowledge is organized in internal representations, whereas the latter provides a basis for interpreting these representations through human conceptual structure. Here, we formally define fundamental representational units (FRUs) and establish two evaluation criteria: fidelity and stability. We further prove that threshold-activated sparse autoencoders (TSAEs) can identify FRUs with high fidelity and stability under appropriate conditions, and validate this result through large-scale experiments. Further analyses reveal that FRUs with higher stability exhibit greater monosemanticity and can be composed across layers to recover semantic organization corresponding to human conceptual structure. These results provide a unified framework for defining, evaluating and identifying FRUs in neural networks, and establish a foundation for characterizing the relationship between neural network representations and human conceptual structure.

However, optimizing targeted biomedical interventions for minimizing spread of HIV often requires observability of the contact network of HIV transmission risk behavior among individuals, which is prohibitively expensive to collect at scale. R2C-Diff positions the peer referral process as incremental corruption of the underlying contact network and learns to reverse it. R2C-Agent plans its interventions over contact networks sampled from R2C-Diff, so its decisions account for uncertainty about the hidden contact network. ##### **Deletion Sensitivity Is a Property of the Model: A Three-Condition Measurement across Seven Open Reasoning Models**
2611.13223v1 by Hanzhang Jia, Liheng Zeng, Zhiyu Yang, Yi Gao, Bo Ma

To the best of our knowledge, this paper presents the first direct, blind, multi-seed measurement of full reasoning-history deletion in multi-turn tool calling, through three rendering conditions, seven open-weight reasoning models, three seeds, 794 tasks, and the official checker. Deletion sensitivity is a model-level property. The reading follows the model itself and stays constant across deployments, and a retrained or re-released model is a new model that a two-cell screen, one retention run and one deletion run, re-reads for the cost of two runs. Deletion sensitivity is thus a measurable property of the model, and the reading must enter routing and aggregation as prior information rather than persist as an invisible default cost.

We introduce AutoBuddy-Bench, a simulator that addresses these challenges. To evaluate current LLMs, we build AutoBuddy, a reference agent with interchangeable backbone LLMs: it identifies tasks from the workspace, grounding each in its source records, and executes each in a separate session with write access. Varying only the backbone, we find a systematic shift in where tasks are lost: weaker models fail mainly at discovery, intermediate models identify tasks but often stop short of carrying them out, and the strongest models fail mainly during execution, most often on business-system operations. Discovery precision is only weakly related to execution quality, suggesting the two are distinct capabilities.

ReGKG-2025 is an archive-derived sample of 281,620 reconstructed article records covering all 1,665 missing 15-minute batches. Articles were recovered from Common Crawl and the Wayback Machine using source quotas estimated from surrounding native GKG data. Text was screened and normalized, non-English bodies were translated, and flagged records were manually reviewed. Eligible articles were converted to the 27-column GKG 2.1 layout. The versioned package includes record-level provenance, archive replay and WARC locators, source summaries, source-by-day calibration weights, and the complete manual decision journal. The sample reaches about 14% of modeled native volume and represents sources accounting for 84% of selected reference record mass. In an intact-week test, 41% of recovered articles matched native GKG records; field agreement on matched pairs is close to the processor baseline (tone MAE 1.69). The dataset supports retrospective content analysis and source-level aggregates; its tone and theme distributions differ from native GKG, and it does not support exhaustive counts. Data: https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.23964265.

摘要：

##### **Least-Privilege Auditing of Agent Tool Calls with Intent Graphs and Reasoning-Guided Escalation**
2611.11305v1 by Doron Shavit

LLM agents that call tools are exposed to indirect prompt injection: instructions hidden in tool outputs can steer an agent into calls the user never asked for. We present a runtime audit that derives an executable intent graph from the user's objective and the deployment's tool catalog with one LLM call per task, and checks every tool call against it with a deterministic loop that makes no LLM call (median 5.7 microseconds per call). Two further mechanisms recover utility. Lookups of the user's own state bypass the audit, because the harm of an attack lies in its write or outward step. The calls that remain flagged are escalated to a reasoning guardian, in the spirit of tool-integrated reasoning models such as START: a reasoning model that sees only the objective, the plan, the call history without tool outputs, and the flagged call, and can verify facts with a restricted Python-expression tool. A current agent showed no baseline attack success, so end-to-end validation remains open.

摘要：

##### **ErosionBench: Probing Alignment Decay in Enterprise Multi-Agent Systems**
2611.11266v1 by Riya Bharathwaj, Akshara Prabhakar, Roshan Ram, Avni Kothari, Sarah Tan, Huan Wang, Silvio Savarese

As enterprises delegate consequential work to multi-agent LLM systems, benign-looking requests can escalate into unsafe actions as they propagate across sessions through poisoned shared state, long-context documents, and trusted peer messages: no single message is malicious, yet the assembled tool action is. Prior benchmarks isolate a single agent under prompt injection or score multi-agent attacks by their final output, leaving cross-session erosion of shared state out of scope. We introduce ErosionBench, an environment-grounded benchmark that executes such attacks and verifies alignment decay at the process level, scoring the realized tool call against authorized intent and provenance with deterministic contracts, not output text. Each scenario is authored as a deterministic state graph before any prose is rendered, so every state change traces to a known cause. It spans four enterprise domains and four decay mechanisms across 1,728 scenarios with matched benign controls, labeled against the OWASP Top-10 for LLM Applications, plus 198 image-based attacks, each paired with a text equivalent, so a defense must survive the same attack in either channel. Perimeter defenses suppress attacks only by over-refusing 54-92% of legitimate work, so a low attack-success rate can conceal severe over-refusal. As a reference defense, we propose a calibrated Meta-Auditor pairing synchronous state checks with asynchronous trajectory monitoring; on a family-disjoint 906-scenario held-out split it reaches 98.5% recall at a 2.9% false-positive rate. Since it monitors trajectory, not surface content, its detection is channel-invariant: the strongest auditor flags image-delivered attacks about as reliably as their text equivalents (recall gap $\leq$2 points per model). Across GPT, Gemini, and Claude auditors, calibration keeps false positives low while recall tracks model capability, strongest for Claude.

摘要：

##### **Beyond Node Attributes: A Controlled Evaluation and Ablation of Local Interaction Statistics for Ethereum Phishing Detection**
2611.11263v1 by Thanh Dat Tran, Duy Loi Duong, Duy Luu Hoang, Minh Huy Tran, Vo Minh Thu Le, Nhat Tung Le

Methods for detecting phishing accounts on Ethereum are usually evaluated as complete pipelines that differ in features, architecture, and training protocol, which makes the contribution of interaction information hard to isolate. This paper evaluates the Unlabeled Feature Extension (UFE), a block of 12 local interaction statistics computed from each account's incoming and outgoing call and transfer edges without reading class labels. UFE is appended to the 14 source features of HEIG-Phish and tested with six model families: Logistic Regression, Random Forest, LightGBM, Graph Attention Network, GraphSAGE, and Meta-IFD. Within each pair of Baseline and Plus variants, the data partition, model configuration, random seed, decision threshold, and sampled neighborhoods are identical; only the input feature block changes. Across 20 predetermined stratified partitions of the same 2,763 labeled externally owned accounts, UFE increased test Macro-F1 by 1.0 to 9.1 percentage points across the six models. Every pair improved on all 20 partitions, all Nadeau-Bengio corrected 95% confidence intervals lie above zero, and the false-positive rate decreased for all six models. An exact-edge Random Forest ablation over 10 predetermined partitions associates most of the gain with the incoming transfer-edge sum attribute; once this feature is present, an incremental contribution of the remaining 11 UFE columns was not established. The results support reporting feature-augmented baselines alongside graph models, but they show stability under re-partitioning of one static, transductive benchmark rather than temporal or cross-dataset generalization.

摘要：

##### **Auto-GlaKG: Automated Biomarker Reasoning for Explainable Glaucoma Detection**
2611.11258v1 by Cheng Huang, Zeyu Han, Yian Hu, Yaopeng Ding, Jingxi Qiu, Yadi Liu

Interpretable glaucoma detection requires a transparent connection between fundus images, clinically meaningful biomarkers, and diagnostic predictions. Knowledge graph (KG) reasoning provides this structure, but performance measured using annotated biomarkers does not establish performance when only images are available. We present Auto-GlaKG, an automated framework that combines image-based biomarker prediction with fixed clinical-prior rules. A ResNet50 network predicts the cup-to-disc ratio (CDR) and six morphological signs using biomarker supervision, and a KG reasoner aggregates their signed rule contributions into a glaucoma score. On 689 fundus images evaluated using stratified, group-aware five-fold cross-validation, Auto-GlaKG achieves a pooled area under the receiver operating characteristic curve (AUC) of 0.951 (95\% confidence interval: 0.931--0.969), with sensitivity of 0.970 and specificity of 0.779. The matched image classifier also achieves an AUC of 0.951, while the rule stage adds 0.2~ms per image. Replacing predicted CDR with its annotation increases fold-mean AUC from 0.953 to 0.995. The framework provides an explicit rule-level account of image-based predictions and identifies CDR estimation as a priority for validation using independently measured biomarkers.

摘要：

##### **MCSA: Multi-Cutoff Spectral Aggregation in SO(3) Equivariant GNNs for Interatomic Potentials**
2611.11226v1 by Junfu Tan, Mengfan Wu, Yu Zhu, Yu Shi, Yifang Qin, Chang Liu, Bonan Zhu, Ziheng Lu

Machine Learning Interatomic Potentials (MLIPs) based on Equivariant Graph Neural Networks (EGNNs) have achieved remarkable progress in atomistic modeling. However, accurately representing atomic interactions across different spatial scales remains challenging. In this work, we investigate the radial spectral properties of SO(3)-equivariant message passing and establish that the cutoff radius controls the characteristic spatial-frequency scale of radial interactions. Based on this analysis, we propose Multi-Cutoff Spectral Aggregation (MCSA), a plug-and-play module that aggregates radial kernels with different cutoff radii to enrich multi-scale geometric representations. To achieve efficient implementation, we exploit the compact support of radial kernels to fuse cutoff-dependent radial weights before angular tensor-product operations, avoiding redundant angular computations while preserving rotational equivariance. Extensive experiments across representative EGNN backbones demonstrate consistent improvements in energy, force, and stress prediction, as well as enhanced accuracy in higher-order properties, including phonon dispersion and lattice thermal conductivity. Further evaluations on layered materials with distinct interaction scales demonstrate the effectiveness of physically motivated cutoff selection. Code is available at: https://github.com/JF-Tan/MCSA.

#### Abstracts
##### **Quantifying the Sampling Frequency Invariance of Time-Series Foundation Models for Sensor Data Applications**
2611.14425v1 by Sourish Gunesh Dhekane, Abinaya Kumar, Torsten Woertwein, Yan-Bo Lin, Thomas Ploetz, Shabnam Ghaffarzadegan

Time-Series Foundation Models (TSFMs) are increasingly used in sensor applications, where sampling frequency (SF) varies across sensors, devices, and operating modes (e.g., power saving). Ideally, TSFMs must remain effective during such SF changes, provided the SF retains task-critical bandwidth. In this paper, we stress-test 4 prominent TSFMs: MOMENT, Chronos-2, Moirai-MoE, and TimesFM, to quantify their SF-invariance and investigate whether their behavior tracks the resulting loss of information bandwidth rather than the SF value itself. Our stress-test probes 3 dimensions: (i) the shift in embeddings, (ii) the degradation in forecasting performance, and (iii) the degradation in downstream performance, quantified by three novel metrics: FSI, FDI, and CDI, respectively. To our knowledge, this is the first study to place SF-invariance at the core of TSFM evaluations. Our results characterize: (i) how SF-invariance depends on the margin between signal's native-SF and the bandwidth its task requires, (ii) how much of that invariance the choice of TSFM explains, and (iii) how much of TSFM's performance degradation under a lower SF reflects information loss rather than a limitation of their abilities. These findings reframe SF-invariance as a measurable, signal-aware property of the (signal X TSFM) pair, and guide practitioners on when TSFMs display SF-invariance.

##### **Bits or permutations? Spending a fixed inference budget on weight precision or on option-order averaging in small local language models**
2611.14413v1 by Mohammed Nayeem Khan, Logesh L, Aliyas Ahmad Misri

Local language models can save memory by storing weights in fewer bits, and they can reduce sensitivity to the order of answer options by averaging a few reorderings. We ask whether the first makes the second worse, and which is the cheaper way to spend a fixed budget. On Qwen3-0.6B and Qwen3-1.7B with simulated round-to-nearest weight quantization (8, 6, 4 and 3 bits, fp16 reference), read from the logits of the letters A to D on ARC-Easy, ARC-Challenge, MMLU and HellaSwag (300 items each), 6 bits lose no accuracy against fp16 (they score +1.8 (+1.0 to +2.5) and +0.9 (+0.3 to +1.5) points higher, which we do not explain), and 8 bits change it by -0.7 (-1.1 to -0.3) and -0.4 (-0.7 to -0.2) points. At 4 bits the answer-slot skew rises from 0.15 to 0.53 (0.6B) and from 0.07 to 0.12 (1.7B); Gaussian noise at the same weight SNR gives 0.15 and 0.37 (two draws) and 0.19 (one draw), so the control is inconclusive on whether rounding adds slot skew beyond equal-power noise. Flip rates rise but stay below an accuracy-matched baseline of independent uniform errors. Averaging four cyclic orders recovers +12.6 points at 4 bits for 0.6B (+5.3 at fp16). We price bits and passes in one machine-independent unit, weight bytes read times passes. In cost-matched pairs, extra orders beat extra bits only when the weights have 6 or more bits, and the decision table reduces to: use 6-bit weights and spend the rest of the budget on up to four orders, one pass each. Everything is simulated on two small models with the simplest quantizer; a title-level search found no earlier study of quantization together with option order.

##### **An Energy-Based Perspective on Machine Learning**
2611.14383v1 by Bo Dai, Yilun Du

Energy-based models (EBMs) define a distribution through a scalar energy, $p_θ(x)\propto\exp(-E_θ(x))$, and are usually presented as one model family among many. This tutorial has a broader goal: beyond introducing EBMs, it uses them as a single lens that connects the different aspects of machine learning. Any EBM is specified by three choices, the energy, which determines what the model represents; the inference procedure, which samples from or optimizes the energy; and the learning method, which fits the energy to data. We first develop these components, including sampling, variational inference, and learning by maximum likelihood, noise contrastive estimation, pseudolikelihood, score matching, and adversarial training, and show that the learning methods share one contrastive structure. We then show that discriminative models, generative models such as GANs, normalizing flows, diffusion and autoregressive models, representation learning methods such as contrastive learning, word2vec, BERT, denoising auto-encoders, and diffusion representations, reinforcement learning with EBM policies and world models, inverse reinforcement learning, and planning, as well as inference-time computation with verifiers, are all EBMs that differ in these three choices, mostly in how they handle the partition function and sampling. This view explains existing methods, organizes them into a common framework, and suggests new algorithms by replacing one component while keeping the others, with composition of energies as a principled way to combine learned models at inference time.

##### **Ninety-Six Ways to Measure a Scribe: Fragmented Evaluation of Ambient Scribes in Healthcare**
2611.14284v1 by Amama Mahmood, Shiye Cao, Laura Fleig, Junan Jiang, Theodore Jensen, Yee-Yin Choong, Kristen Greene, Chien-Ming Huang

Ambient artificial intelligence (AI) scribes have moved rapidly from experimental pilots to enterprise-wide deployment across health systems, promising reduced documentation burden and improved clinician experience. While research evaluating these tools has expanded quickly, there are no agreed-upon protocols or standardized metrics for assessing their performance, making it difficult to compare findings or cumulatively synthesize evidence across studies. Across 54 recent evaluation studies, we identify ten evaluation dimensions and 40 distinct metrics, operationalized in 96 different ways-35 of them (36%) used by only a single study. Assessment is dominated by documentation efficiency and time, measured in 44 of 54 studies. The other dimensions appear at broadly similar rates (16-24 studies each), and safety and clinical outcome least consistently of all. Ripple effects on the broader care system, such as impact on patients' care outcomes, redistribution of work, changes in billing and coding intensity, and downstream use of notes by other clinicians are rarely assessed. Moreover, the metrics used rely heavily on self-report and are fragmented and inconsistently operationalized, which limits comparability and hinders synthesis across studies. As an effort to support more rigorous and cumulative evaluation, in this paper we review the study designs, data sources, and metrics commonly used to evaluate ambient AI scribes in healthcare settings, discuss the limitations of current evaluation practice, and argue for a more holistic approach grounded in a standardized, multi-dimensional set of metrics.

##### **Visual Similarity in Adult Word Extension and Artificial Word Learning**
2611.14116v1 by Patricio M. Vera

When two objects share a name, how much does their visual similarity explain, and what do the teaching examples add? First, we reanalyzed trial-level data from a large adult word-extension study (Lewis & Frank, 2018), computing each test image's distance to the examples shown in the space of a frozen self-supervised visual encoder (DINOv2). Distance was associated with extension (log odds -3.23 per standard deviation; 520 people), and an exploratory post hoc analysis suggested that this association persisted within the taught domain after controlling for taxonomic level and teaching condition. Example composition also mattered: after three examples, adults extended a word to 1.1% of superordinate items when the examples were subordinate and to 77.5% when they were superordinate. Second, in an artificial learner using the same encoder, words joining two categories were learned less accurately the farther apart the categories were (84.7%, 65.4%, and 53.0% naming accuracy for near, mid, and far pairs). The encoder's distances correlated with human similarity and confusability measures from three datasets (rho = .501-.795), but this representational alignment did not translate into conclusive evidence that human-derived distances predict learner difficulty, and a second encoder trained on one child's experience reproduced the tier ordering but not the pair-level effect. The secondary human data were analyzed under an internal plan fixed before the confirmatory analyses (not externally preregistered). Visual similarity accounts for part of how generalization is organized in both systems, while the composition of examples contributes what the distance we used does not capture.

##### **Can KV Cache Be Reused Across Model Updates? A DeepSeek-V4-Flash Case Study**
2611.14051v1 by Zhikun Tao, Fangyong Jin, Junping Zhao

Reusing key--value (KV) caches across language model updates could avoid repeated computation of shared context, but changes in model weights raise a fundamental question about what these caches preserve. We investigate direct cache reuse across four model updates, with a detailed study of DeepSeek-V4-Flash to Flash-0731. Our central finding is that caches can retain useful task information without faithfully reproducing the updated model's behavior. Retrieval accuracy varies substantially across updates, ranging from near-native performance to large losses. With source caches already available, reuse achieves median time-to-first-token speedups of $2.10$--$5.08\times$ on three longer-prefix QA benchmarks. To characterize the behavioral differences behind these outcomes, we introduce Functional Cache Divergence, which compares next-token distributions under reused and native caches. Direct KV cache reuse better preserves native model behavior in natural-text continuation, whereas compatibility degrades with increasing prefix length in single-token retrieval tasks. Answer-margin analyses help explain how correct retrieval decisions survive substantial internal state changes. Yet even agreement on the first generated token does not ensure agreement in answer content or stopping behavior. These findings establish task utility and fidelity to native inference as distinct criteria for evaluating cross-version KV cache reuse.

##### **Interleaving anatomical inspection and diagnostic reasoning for explainable radiology report generation**
2611.14018v1 by Chentao Huang, Runlong Lin, Zhijun Wang, Yunxiang Li, Yuanyuan Sun, Jian Wang, Hongfei Lin, Ling Luo

Automated radiology report generation can draft clinically plausible reports, yet the pathway from visual evidence to reported findings remains opaque, leaving the diagnostic basis of these reports unexplained and the generated reports difficult to review or revise. We introduce ARIVE (Anatomy-guided Reasoning with Interleaved Visual Evidence), an explainable report generation framework that interleaves anatomical inspection with diagnostic reasoning prior to final report composition. To support this process, we constructed 159,416 multimodal chain-of-thought (MCoT) trajectories from MIMIC-CXR, wherein visual evidence from inspected anatomical regions informs subsequent clinical interpretation. Trained on these trajectories and further optimized using anatomical and clinical-content rewards, ARIVE achieved superior clinical-efficacy performance compared with strong baselines on MIMIC-CXR and the abnormality-centered MIMIC-ABN. ARIVE's reports were competitive with those of leading models in blinded expert grading, and its MCoT trajectories earned high ratings for diagnostic traceability. Furthermore, a paired review evaluation suggests that access to MCoT can aid in identifying errors within generated reports.

##### **A Stage-Wise Explainable Pipeline for Arteriolar-to-Venular Ratio Estimation and Retinopathy Screening**
2611.14013v1 by Sharan Sharmeela Karuppannan, Yoga Sri Varshan, Subin Sahayam, Umarani Jayaraman

The arteriolar-to-venular diameter ratio (AVR) is a clinically motivated marker of retinal vascular health. We present a five-stage pipeline for AVR estimation and exploratory retinopathy screening from fundus images, with inspectable intermediate outputs at each stage. The pipeline combines vessel and artery-vein masks, optic-disc localization, vessel-calibre estimation using a minimal-chord boundary walk, and AVR computation from dominant arterial and venular branches. An explicit threshold-based rule produces the screening decision, enabling errors to be traced to individual processing stages. On an exploratory cohort of 30 HRF images, we report 96.7% accuracy, an F1-score of 0.968 and ROC-AUC of 0.978. Ablation studies and an exploratory comparison with VascX examine the effects of vessel pairing, calibre measurement and aggregation. Interpretation is limited by the small cohort, proxy disease labels and absence of independent reference calibre measurements. The pipeline provides a transparent framework for investigating retinal vascular biomarkers, with further validation needed on larger, clinically graded cohorts.

##### **The Case for AI-Enabled Gate Monitoring as Farm Security in the UK: A PRISMA-Guided Review of the Evidence on Rural Crime, Livestock Predation and Farm Access**
2611.13933v1 by Steve Symes, Paul Fergus, Mark Carr, Omar McIver, Arwyn Jones

Farm security in the UK rests on thinner evidence than its cost suggests. We ran a PRISMA guided review: twelve searches of Web of Science and Scopus on 2nd -4th November 2026 returned 1,183 records (761 unique), and grey-literature and hand-searches added 35 assessed sources. We synthesised 96 database records and 24 grey sources narratively. UK peer-reviewed evidence is small (16 records), and the main UK survey of crime and farmer mental health has 80 responses. Cost estimates rest on insurer claims: NFU Mutual put UK rural crime at £41.5 million in 2025, with livestock theft up almost 30% to about £4.5 million and dog attacks on livestock costing £1.95 million. The Home Office states that agricultural crime cannot be separately identified in its central data. We found no independent evaluation of cameras, sensors, trackers or marking; light and sound deterrents lost effect within one to five months in a systematic review of anti-predator interventions; and no study examined how often farm gates are left open or what follows. Camera-trap AI papers report accuracy on ecological datasets, none at a farm gate in the records we found, and in one comparison machine learning detected 41% of wildlife against 81% for analysts. We argue, as an inference from these gaps and not as a demonstrated effect, that continuous gate-level AI-mediated monitoring, as implemented in Fieldmark AI, would be highly beneficial for UK farm security. It would create the farm-level, time-stamped record that no official source holds, verify events that periodic human checks miss, resolve whether foxes, dogs or people were responsible, and give every other security measure something to be tested against. No study has yet shown that it reduces loss, and we set out the evaluation needed to show it.

##### **Beyond Proxy Metrics: MLLMs as Human Surrogates for Explainable AI Evaluation**
2611.13926v1 by Eoin M. Kenny, Salim I. Amoukou, Tom Bewley

Explainable AI (XAI) has expanded into a broad and impactful research area, but the fundamental question of how best to evaluate explanation techniques has proven frustratingly elusive. Methodologies typically revolve around either proxy metrics which tell us little about the utility of an explanation, or human evaluation which---while generally considered the gold standard---is expensive, difficult to scale, and challenging to reproduce. In this paper, we investigate if Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) can act as surrogate human participants to automate the evaluation of XAI. To do this, we first propose a ``purpose-grounded'' evaluation framework with metrics applicable to both MLLMs and human users alike. Then, we offer some conceptual insights as to why and when we can trust these models to automate this process. Lastly, we conduct extensive empirical testing across five purpose-grounded areas such as model improvement and fourteen popular MLLMs. Results indicate that all models trend positively, but OpenAI and Qwen models generally show higher alignment compared to other variants, which would be the recommended choices in practice. We expect our insights not to replace user testing, but rather to help XAI researchers complement their current evaluation methodologies and move the field away from heavy reliance on proxy metrics for measuring explanation utility.

##### **Softness is something you also need! Why compliance helps control: information metrics of a passive walker with compliant ankle**
2611.13779v1 by Fabio Bonsignorio, Elisabetta Barletta, Sorin Dragomir, Francesco Esposito, Enrica Zereik

A great gap in adaptivity, flexibility, and dexterity still exists between intelligent robots, whether they are made of rigid or (partially) compliant components, and their naturally evolved animal and vegetal counterparts. One reason could be of `paradigm' nature. On the one hand, most robots (both rigid and soft) are still designed by following a `Cartesian' approach, with top-down control and a clear mind-body separation. Designing artificial agents on the basis of the principles that we observe in biology is not an easy task. The principles of organization of animals and plants rely on morphological computation and emergence of fit behaviors from loosely coupled networks of embodied agents.
  Modeling and control require radically new approaches. In this paper we briefly recall and explain some seminal mathematical results linking information metrics and dynamics of the agents, already published years ago; we integrate them with some recent methods developed by us and by the community, we highlight some brief remarks about their relevance and we hint to our current and near future work towards finding concrete and efficient ways for the modeling and control of completely or partially compliant artificial agents and for a better understanding of physical intelligence in nature. We present a technical note summarizing phase-space volume calculations for inverted-pendulum and passive-dynamic walking models, following our proposed framework. Starting from the McGeer compass gait, we introduce knee-ankle compliance, foot-rocker effects, and a Ruina-style compass gait with distributed leg inertia. Standard heel-strike maps are derived and used in the estimation of viable phase-space volumes $W(γ)$, based on a state-dependent Monte-Carlo and Reproducing Kernel Hilbert Spaces methods. We show how the methods based on RKHS allow deeper and more subtle analysis.

##### **A Concept-Based Interpretable Framework for Clinical Text-based Prediction via Token-Concept Attention without Concept Supervision**
2611.13564v1 by Jifan Gao, Junjie Hu, John Caskey, Madeline Oguss, Simona Cristea, Anoop Mayampurath, Matthew Churpek, Majid Afshar, Guanhua Chen

Objective: Concept-based interpretation has emerged as a promising approach for explaining blackbox models using human-understandable, high-level concepts. However, most existing methods require annotated concept labels to train the model to identify concepts in the input, and obtaining such annotations in clinical text is often expensive. This study aims to enable concept-based interpretation for language models without concept supervision on clinical prediction tasks. Methods: We propose a novel framework that enables concept-based interpretation without concept supervision through a tailored cross-attention bottleneck. Clinical text and the text descriptions of medical concepts are first tokenized and embedded by a pretrained language model. A cross-attention module then estimates the presence of each concept for each text token, producing concept relevance scores that capture how concepts are involved throughout the note. These scores are pooled and used in a linear prediction layer to model clinical outcomes in an interpretable manner. Results: The performance of the proposed framework was compared against blackbox models across two tasks: chest trauma severity stratification using a private dataset and an in-hospital mortality prediction task using the publicly available MIMIC-III dataset. The proposed framework achieved AUROC values within 0.02 of the corresponding blackbox models. The framework also enabled cohort- and patient-level interpretation, capturing the overall concept importance across the cohort and patient-specific concept contributions. Conclusion: Our proposed framework realizes concept-based interpretation without requiring ground-truth concept presence labels. It improves the transparency of clinical text predictions while maintaining strong performance in clinical prediction tasks.

##### **Massive Activations Confound Sparse Autoencoders**
2611.13548v1 by Nathan Delisle, Chenhao Tan

Sparse autoencoders (SAEs) are commonly evaluated by variance explained and ablation damage. We show how massive activations undermine both evaluations, and propose a correction. Massive activations cause up to 99.9% of activation variance to lie along the first principal component (PC1). Because the PC1 component has no unique sparse decomposition, each SAE run rebuilds it from a different set of features. These features dominate importance rankings: an average of 47 of the 50 most important features are unmatched in a separate run, compared with about 27 of a random 50. A dictionary can also report 99.96% of variance explained while leaving 33% of the variance outside PC1 unexplained. We bypass PC1 by training the SAE on activations with PC1 removed, then adding PC1 to the reconstruction. In affected models, the top-50 feature sharing rate rises from 4-39.5% to 73-99.5%. At the same TopK sparsity, the resulting SAE degrades the model up to 44% less and reduces content reconstruction error by up to 33.9%.
